ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-15.ana.ud2166e1874	Let me first start by thanking the rapporteur for a clean and good job in the work on the message and the settings. I would also like to thank the Committee for good cooperation, although we obviously do not agree on all parts of this message. Report. St. 25 for 2014–2015, On the right track, represents a milestone in Norwegian transport policy. For the first time in many years, substantial efforts are being made to reduce planning time and build faster. Looking at developments in Norwegian road construction, we can establish the following: we have too long planning time. It takes on average approx. ten years to plan a road project. Secondly, we have a lack of predictability. Projects are structured in pieces and divided rather than in a coherent and comprehensive way, and there is a lack of predictability in funding. It's not an effective way to plan and build a road. Thirdly, we have a neglected maintenance. The maintenance run on the road network is formidable and has increased every year until now. I think there is a relatively large consensus that these are key challenges in Norwegian road construction. The opposition, which until October 2013 had government responsibility, also appears in its comments to recognise that these are challenges and ‘are open to, and want to welcome, new and smarter models for road construction’. [[...]] But there is a stop to the constructive approach. Rather than making concrete suggestions on how to build the road faster, the Workers' Party and the Centre are only concerned with criticising the solutions proposed by the government. They therefore agree with the diagnosis, but lack their own solutions. Increased investment on the road often ends in a discussion of how much money we spend for different purposes. It's important. Without adequate appropriations, there is not enough road to be built either. In its budgets for 2014 and 2015, the government has pledged both on the road and on the road. This escalation means that we are in front of the form to overfill the NTP, both in dollars and in the number of projects. In addition, we have raised the commitment to maintenance, which means that for the first time in several decades we reduce the maintenance run, both on the highway and on the railway. Where the decay increased under the red-green regime and we actually had a real decline in the funds available for maintenance, we now see a positive development and a reduced lag. Increased appropriations, however, are not enough. We also need to make sure that we get as much money back as possible by reducing the planning time – a halve is the goal – and building faster and smarter. And that's what this message is essentially about. How can we best use the means we use for transportation purposes? The previous government boasted that it was spending more money than they promised, building less roads than they promised. This government is going to measure how much we're building, not just how much money we're using. The message defines three main actions — firstly the establishment of a road development company, secondly the increased use of public–private cooperation, OPS, and thirdly a toll reform. The establishment of a development company is an important step in creating greater predictability and achieving more holistic, coherent and rational development. The company is being financed with a share of the road tax and public budget appropriations. In addition, the company may use tolls in the financing basis. The company will also have a limited loan facility, which could help to ensure steady progress during the construction period. The development company will have a start-up portfolio that mainly includes important transport corridors in and between our most populous areas and where there is mainly a basis for four-field roads. It means important distances on both E18, E39 and E6. The portfolio is approx. 130 B.C.E. kr measured in today's value and to be expanded within a 20-year perspective, or preferably faster. Prioritisation of the routes to be allocated to the company will take place in the NTP process. Thus, the road carrier will be seen in conjunction with the overall priorities of the national transport plan, and we will ensure a good balance, for the benefit of the whole country. Since the company will be responsible for longer and continuous sections, it will be up to the company itself to define the order of development. The policy will focus on the overall priorities that professionals are building on. It is also important that the company has total responsibility. It will stimulate smart choice of solutions, providing a good economy in a life-cycle perspective. I record that the opposition is concerned that the establishment of a road company will lead to a fragmentation of the trade unions. I don't think there's any reason for that. We'll have two qualitatively good trade unions building roads that will challenge each other in a positive way and stimulate innovation. And then it's important that these professional environments also cooperate where it's natural. The opposition is also concerned about increasing red tape. But why would that be? After all, the number of employees has to be scaled up according to the tasks to be undertaken and the portfolio of employees in the company at all times, while the choice of contract form will also have an impact on the number of employees the company will need. There should also be no parallel organisations. When the Road Service gives up responsibility for a stretch, they don't have to have anyone working on it. When the company is given responsibility for complete and coherent sections, it will be easier to dispose of its own resources efficiently compared to the piece-by-part thinking for which the red-greens have been exponents for many years. Public-private cooperation, or OPS, has proved to be an effective implementation strategy. Seeing planning, implementation and maintenance in conjunction over a 25-year period will stimulate smarter investment solutions from a life-cycle perspective. We have a good example of this from the construction of the route between Klett and Orkanger, where the contractor was early in choosing LED lighting, simply because it gave a better operating economy. This is not a big deal, but it illustrates the point. OPS is an implementation strategy. By outsourcing road sections to a private operator that is also responsible for operation and maintenance, the risk of overruns is transferred to the private operator and the State will have a predictable standard on the road during the contract period. This also means that the company will have strong incentives to implement the project as quickly as possible. I record again that the opposition still refers to the disadvantages of private financing for road development, and that the state pays down above, for example. 25 years. So either the representatives of the Workers' Party and the Centre have not read the big message, or they will not understand it. The model to which the government now submits is to cultivate OPS as an implementation strategy by paying the principal weight of the contract amount early in the contract period, after completion of the road. In this way, the disadvantage is reduced by private financing more expensive than the state, while at the same time taking out all the benefits of OPS as an effective implementation strategy – that is, the best from two worlds. We have the tradition of establishing one toll company per road project. This has led to the fact that today it is approx. 60 toll companies in Norway. We want to reduce the number of toll companies to approx. three to five incorporated companies. The aim is to facilitate increased professionalisation, reduce administrative and financing costs and facilitate increased user-friendliness for the drivers. Once we have chosen to use tolls as an important element in the financing of road projects, it is only fair and reasonable that we also facilitate that the drivers experience the scheme as fair and easy to deal with. An important step to achieve this is to separate the issuer role from the toll company and to facilitate more commercial issuers. Here you can find a variety of solutions that will make everyday life easier for the drivers. The government also has an ambition to establish an interest-rate compensation scheme for toll loans. It is a tool to reduce fares or reduce the amount of depreciation to the benefit of the drivers. It will also be an important element to stimulate the pooling of toll companies, as it is a condition for accessing the scheme. I record that we were not able to clarify all the principal aspects of it now, but we are going to take it back in the context of the 2016 state budget. Finally, it is unnatural to talk about a road reform without getting into the hub of Norwegian road construction. The state road system is naved in the road construction today and will be in the future as well. Even if we establish a road company, the main gravity of the NTP projects will still be implemented by the State Road Administration. That's why it's important to clarify that this means that we're still going to have a strong state road service. Instead of having a professional environment dedicated to road construction, we will now have two professional environments, which complement and challenge each other, and which will cooperate where appropriate. The sum of the grips presented in this message, along with the good professional environment we have in the State Road Service, will in the years to come help us use our travel money smarter. This is not a magic formula for more money, but it's a good grip to build a more effective road than we do today, and it's not one day too early.

On the contrary – we need to build more, better and smarter. The next generation of vehicles is going to be nearly zero-emission, and then it makes no sense not to invest any more in infrastructure. The average car on Norwegian roads is currently a 2004 model, which emits 180 grams of CO2 per km. The average emissions for new cars sold in 2015 are slightly above 90 grams of CO2 per km, i.e. about half of the emissions. When we renewed the car fleet in 2025 so that the average car is a 2015 model and not a 2004 model, it will have a major impact on local emissions. If we are able to combine this with better roads that lead to fewer traffic accidents, better traffic flows, less driving and fewer local emissions, we will have a better functioning society. Before, the most important thing has been to put in place a new bridge or tunnel or to upgrade a few kilometres of the motorway from two fields to maybe three fields, then lead the main road back into towns and cities, where the road has always gone. With today's decisions, we want to stimulate a sharp reduction in the number of bomb companies, so that operating, administrative and financing costs will be reduced. This reduction will benefit drivers, either in the form of small tolls, shorter downtime or higher discounts for those who choose to use bombs. They have not promoted a single proposal to stimulate bombers to participate voluntarily in the toll reform, while we have proposed an interest-rate compensation scheme. We want it to give an incentive to the bomb companies by allowing the state to pay some of the bomb companies' borrowing costs if they choose to become part of the reform. For many companies, this can be a huge saving. Earlier this year, VG could show that you can get up to 30 pst. a discount on some projects with government proposals. Rogfast, a tunnel project in Rogaland and part of the ferry-free E39, would get a bomb fare of nearly 350 kroner with red-green assumptions. So the red-greens have no proposals, and their toll reform would break – if that was what we were going to adopt today. With the Progress Party in government, we will now have a road development company in place to ensure more comprehensive planning and development of major road projects and more efficient use of taxpayers' money, and we will see that the toll burden goes slowly, but surely in a better direction.

ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-15.ana.ud2166e2639	Investment in infrastructure is one of the most important activities a society can do to facilitate a good framework for business and population. It is also an investment for future generations. Just as we today have the joy of using roads, tunnels and bridges that generations before us have lifted up and built, generations that come after us will enjoy the investments and promises that we make, among other things. in the case under consideration today. The road reform marks a shift, a willingness to think new and to try new structures, both to reduce the planning time and to get away from this piece by piece and piece development practice, which has been discussed earlier in this debate, both in terms of planning and in terms of funding. This government has ambitions to build the country. We have committed ourselves in the government platform to over-comply with the national transport plan, and we have committed ourselves to establishing a separate road company to help achieve a more comprehensive and coherent development of the main road network in Norway. This road carrier shall also contribute to greater cost-effectiveness in these deployments. The company will have all the possibilities for long-term planning and overall implementation of projects through the 20-year time-frame for the start-up portfolio adopted by the Stortinget today. This government is committed to the organisation of infrastructure that will link larger areas of residence and work together. In this way, as a society, we are able to exploit a greater potential for the creation of value added and the efficient exploitation of society’s growth potential, and this is also emphasised in the context of the starting-up portfolio selected. Therefore, I am pleased that the route between Kristiansand and Stavanger is within the starting portfolio of the road company. The Kristiansand and Stavanger regions are two growth areas that have a lot of industry and business activities. Shorter travel time will strengthen the cooperation and exploitation of the competence of strong business clusters. The labour market is given greater access to labour when travel time is reduced and efficient roads reduce transport costs and businesses become more competitive. Areas between these two cities will also benefit greatly from a more efficient road link and shorter travel time. This increases the picture of opportunities, both for new business start-ups and for job choices. The government is working for increased road safety, and unfortunately there are too many accidents along this heavily trafficked route. I therefore look forward to the realization and hope and believe that this route will be among the priorities, even in time, within the 20-year horizon set for the implementation of the new road company.

ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-15.ana.ud2166e2851	On 12 May, the government presented Report. St. 27 for 2014-2015 On the right track – rail sector reform. This is probably an example of good cooperation between the two government parties and the Christian People's Party and the Left. It's essentially amazing that one minority government manages to unleash one major reform after another through the Storting. This can only be done when the willingness to change is present with all the partners. If it's not, it's going to be a minimum common multiple. For me as the case rapporteur, it became quite soon clear that the basis for common comments was microscopic – and that was the case. In short, the consensus is limited to who is in the committee and that we are dealing with the same matter. The last thing you can wonder about is if you follow the public debate. However, as the rapporteur, I would like to point out that the opposition uses long observations to mention that the matter is being dealt with in five weeks. This has been assessed by the Committee and also by the Presidency, and the majority has chosen to promote the reform of the Stortinget. The railway reform points out a direction and gives some details about where we want to go, and with what organisation. It has been announced since we joined, that competition in passenger train traffic is coming, so there can be no surprise. Many issues in the reform remain open and need to find their solutions in the further work. The majority therefore believe that it was important and appropriate to deal with the reform before the summer, so that the concrete solutions can be found and the reorganisation started. In the further work, we invite all interested parties to join in and discuss the concrete solutions that are feasible, which are the most effective, and which safeguard the employees of the sector and the competences that are there. In the following, I will use the time to present the majority's comments. There I can rejoice that the Right Party, the Advance Party, the Christian People's Party and the Left stand together on all the remarks. Why a railway reform? The short version is as follows: In some areas, the division of tasks in the railway sector is unclear and inappropriate. The railway works are both governmental bodies and operate lines on a daily basis. Responsibility for railway property is shared between Rome Eiendom and the Railway Agency in a way that creates problems. The NSB, as one of several commercial actors, is operating route planning against regional authorities. The Ministry of Transport purchases passenger transport services directly from the NSB as its sole provider. Thus, costs cannot be tested on the market. We here in the hall, which grants money for the purchase of transport services to halve the cost of what a train ticket actually costs, don't know if we could get an even better offer for the travellers with others. There are weak incentive mechanisms for the Railway Agency to keep the network open for traffic and for the NSB to drive innovation and efficiency, in relation to whether they had to compete with other train companies. It is too little overall and long-term control, for example. low focus on rail policy objectives related to the transport provision to be provided by the rail system in the short and long term. There is also a lack of focus on whether the measures taken are the most effective to achieve the objectives. There has been too much focus on individual projects, single departures and stations. Everything was not better before – most of it is better now: more trains, more departures and better trains. There has never been more money allocated to the railway than now. However, despite increasing appropriations, delays and operational problems are almost daily. Not everyone is so big that they get media attention, but they are noticed by the travelers. I'm thinking, it has to be possible to do better. Yes, says the department, yes, says the railway manager, and yes, says the group manager of the NSB. That is why we have a rail reform to deal with in Stortinget today. Hopefully, it will help solve both the major challenges and the small ones. How can we get the money that is granted to go a little bit further? How can we make sure that the money goes to the right projects? It's about organizing better, building up artificial barriers and cultivating the distinction between authority, builder and carrier. It is liberating that the Railway Agency and the NSB have seen this and have themselves made useful input into the railway reform. So does the Flytrain. The opponents of the reform spend a lot of energy finding examples of what is not working in other countries. This is actually a derailment, because the communication clearly states that the new reform must be adapted to Norwegian conditions. We have fewer inhabitants, live more widely, and the country is, when true, not specially organized for public transport. Therefore, it is foreseen that the railway should remain a public responsibility and that the public should have ownership of all infrastructure, including train equipment. So how can it be cleaned up in this? The government points to three main elements, which are supported by our partners, the Christian People's Party and the Left: It is necessary to distinguish clearly between controlling and performing entities by the fact that the tasks of authority are grouped together in purely administrative bodies without the provision of services. The reform proposes a new railway directorate responsible for long-term governance through, for example: capacity analysis, development of route models, socio-economic analyses, overall investigation, planning and budgetary work and coordination of resources in the sector and, in addition, the task of purchasing passenger transport services by train, which is currently carried out by the Ministry of Transport and new tasks arising from the reform. This applies, inter alia, to: strengthening the involvement of regional actors in the sector and ensuring contract management in new areas. It is proposed that the infrastructure undertaking responsible for managing existing and building new railway infrastructure should be responsible for traffic management and the management and development of property. The entity shall manage the clear responsibility of the State for the infrastructure and shall have a commercial device. The government will gather and cultivate the environment which will have as its primary task the construction and operation of the railway. Parts of the Railway Station will be continued in a state-owned enterprise. Rome Property is transferred to the State enterprise to collect the property management in one location in the sector. It will provide better opportunities to create more effective nodes. The business organisation of the infrastructure also provides for multi-annual agreements between the country and the infrastructure undertaking, which will increase predictability and overall use of the resources. The Right Party, the Advance Party, the Christian People's Party and the Left will point out that there is a need to improve railway infrastructure. That is why it is part of the reform. It is the main task of the new infrastructure undertaking to expand and maintain the infrastructure. This applies to both rails and other properties. By providing infrastructure managers with appropriate framework conditions, operational freedom and clearly defined tasks to train operators, the infrastructure undertaking will be better able to perform this task. By facilitating competition for passenger trains, as is already the case for freight trains, this will benefit both individuals and businesses through better services. The sum of this will help to better meet the needs of the customer – individuals and businesses – than they do today. The NSB is continued as a combined transport company, all-time owned by the State, consisting of NSB Persontog, CargoNet, Train Company AB, Nettbuss and NSB Gjøvikbanen. But NSB Persontog will eventually meet competition in the same way as CargoNet, the Train Company and Nettbuss do today. Among other things, the Flytrain will be given the opportunity to become an active actor in addition to the accommodation service for the Gardermoen. If you ask those who work in Nettbuss, they know that winning a bidding round implies a time-limited right and obligation to transport passengers on the allocated route. That's not the same thing as having a monopoly on the distance. Reform should have something to go for. In the Storing Message, the government announces its visions for the Norwegian railway system. Firstly, the railway must be at the heart of the solution to the transport challenges. Norway is facing significant transport challenges in the coming years. Population growth, especially around major cities, and the growth of freight transport must be managed in an efficient and environmentally friendly way. Railways must be a central part of the solution to this challenge and, therefore, provision must be made for rail to become an integral part of the development of traffic hubs and urban environments. It is also necessary to facilitate the movement of goods from road to sea and rail. Secondly, the railway service must continue to be a public responsibility. Decisions on where to go and on how often to go should continue to be taken by bodies under political control, which meet the needs of society, users and customers, and which look to more than business-economy assessments. Responsibility for the railway infrastructure shall lie with public owned activities. Thirdly, the customer shall be placed in the centre. Unclear and varying customer focus contributes to the lack of sufficient incentives for the customer to be at the centre of the organisation’s priorities. The railway works have no economic self-interest in keeping train traffic going. Low road tax means that the Railway Agency does not appear to be a supplier with clear obligations towards the rail companies. The NSB, which has delimited monopoly, has a weaker incentive to develop a better product for the customer than if the company competed with others. Fourthly, long-term objectives must guide development. The government proposes to continue today's division of responsibilities model. However, it is important that regional and local authorities and municipalities are more involved than at present in the planning of train traffic. The county authorities are responsible for the majority of public transport in this country, and it is therefore important that the new directorate takes this into account. Fifthly, it must be organised for competition. One of the key steps in the submitted rail reform is to facilitate competition for tracks through the following measures: it seeks to ensure that State-owned companies do not benefit from competitive advantages through special advantages or control over strategic inputs. A solution is outlined in which some defined sections are exposed to competition. The public imposes requirements applicable to Norwegian conditions. Operators may dispose of Norwegian material unless otherwise agreed. No one is questioning the NSB and the competence of its employees. On the contrary, the NSB is a capable carrier, and they should still be. It's interesting that the NSB today, through the Train Company, runs in Sweden after winning competitions there. During these days, the company competes for a contract that has more customers than NSB has in Norway. I think many in the debate have shown a great distrust of the NSB when they think the NSB will lose all assignments and disappear. No one has better conditions than those who run trains in Norway today to win competition. The government is making some important limitations in the reform. The notification does not assess how the railway network should develop in the coming years. The railway reform also does not deal with the development or closure of sections or changes to the offer of trains. These are assessments which have to be made primarily as part of the national transport plan. The Right Party, the Advance Party, the Christian People's Party and the Left therefore find a frame-up of concrete railway actions to be outside the scope of this case. The same applies to the flight train AS. The debate focuses on the chapters of employee organisations dealing with the possible competitive outsourcing of train sections. As a train passenger, I was given to pilots about how dangerous the reform is. However, the content is strongly coloured by the political position of the organisations and without any real anchoring in the main message. Here are some examples: it is manufactured as if competitive outsourcing would replace public procurement. That's not right. Collective transport will continue to be subsidised independently of the operator. The competition is not about the customer, but about the state's money, it is claimed. The answer to that is that the reform is about how we can achieve the best possible train offer for the money we actually spend. The NSB and the Flytog give the state 900 million. kr in dividends, it was – understood that it is stupid to get rid of the NSB and the Fly train. The reform does not mean getting rid of the NSB and the Flytrain. Both will still be public property. Moreover, the purpose of the reform is not to maximise State profits, but to ensure optimal train provision. The infrastructure is very poor and causes 70 pst. of all train delays. It is quite right that the main challenges on the railways are due to poor infrastructure. The railway has not tolerated the red-green decay to which it was exposed for eight years, where the maintenance seat dredged almost doubled from 9 billion. NOK to nearly 18 billion. kr. But we have ambitions for 100 pst. by the railway. If 30 pst. by rail, better organisation can be achieved, there is indeed good reason to start the reform process. There has been a lot of focus on competitive outsourcing. It's not unexpected, but it's a little strange anyway, because it's quite common in public transport. In our area, there is no resistance to the fact that Router AS has been offering bus routes for seven to eight years. The question is, does it pay off? TØI carried out an en route assessment of the competitive outsourcing of the Gjøvik Railway. The conclusion is interesting: the operating costs are on average 12 pst. lower in the period 2007-2009 compared to the estimated cost in 2015. Thank you for your attention.

After the Nordlandsbanen was completed in 1962, no new railway sections were built in Norway until the Oslotunnel was completed in 1980, and the Gardermobanen in 1999 – two new 40 year routes. The 1970s and 1980s were the decade of private cars in Norway. As I said, in 1999, the Gardermoban was finished as Norway’s first high-speed rail. And why is it that large railway countries such as Germany, Austria, France and Switzerland believe that the coordination of train traffic, infrastructure and route planning is the solution to generate growth for the railways? Research also shows that countries with integrated railways consistently perform better results in terms of traffic growth and better train provision than countries that have split up the railways. Experience from all European countries is that there are not primarily organisational changes, but the willingness to use money on the railways to decide whether the railways are going well or not. We now have both a road reform and a rail reform. We need to expand a lot of new infrastructure in the coming years, and we need to take better care of what we have already built. Then we need a more general and long-term management of the sector than we have today. We keep the authority tasks of the Railway Agency. Today, the Railway Agency has too much tension in its tasks. The Directorate will have a central position in the management and not least in the coordination of the sector, by, for example, being the purchaser of infrastructure and development services as well as the purchaser of train transport. We are setting up something completely new – an infrastructure company. Yeah, that's a heavy word, but this is just a beginning. A wide range of operational tasks will be carried out here. The entity will have a task as a provider of functional railway infrastructure and will provide the building owner with the function of important development projects. The entity shall be granted operational freedom and incentives for efficient operation. In shaping the rail sector of the future, it is important that we include all good forces in society, both public and private. Private suppliers are being used in the railway sector today. This is good, but we believe that the possibility of providing us with more innovative and efficient transport services needs to be exploited more. We do this in most other parts of the transport sector, with good results. The Gjøvik Railway is currently the only passenger train section with which several actors have been invited. Experiences from this route are very good. This is something we want to develop further to other parts of passenger traffic and passenger train network. One of our main reasons for doing this is that we wanted the searchlight to be more focused on the customer's needs. We want a more customized train offer, and experience from other social sectors shows us that getting more service providers into a diversity gives us the best possible service innovation. We want more people living around the big cities to want to use the trains. If this is to be done, we have to make sure that train provision is so flexible and customer-oriented that it fits into people's everyday lives. We believe that the track until several train users do not go through regulation and coercion, but by providing train providers with the best conditions to compete. Then it's up to them to make their offer attractive to most people — including co-operating a ticketing system. Too many people want the car and road to be the only possible practical means of transport in their everyday lives. A more flexible train service that puts customer needs first will lead to far more than today preferring the train to its daily transport, of its own free will. We are betting on carrots and not whips, simply because the train provides the transport services most efficiently.

ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-15.ana.ud2166e3133	Considerable population growth is expected in Norway over the next decades. This creates an increased need for transport. Not least in and around the largest cities. We have a great untapped potential on the freight side, and with the shortening of travel time by rail between several of the larger cities, the railway will appear as an attractive alternative also on the longer distances. In the climate agreement, we have committed ourselves to taking the growth of passenger transport in metropolitan areas with public transport, bicycles and corridors. In this context, and in the interests of accessibility and rail as a powerful and land-efficient means of transport, the train appears to be the central solution to the transport challenges of the future. That is why the Christian People's Party supports this proposal for rail reform – because we want to make the transport offer represents the railway, more important for more customers on the passenger and freight side. At the same time, it is important on the part of the Christian People's Party to stress what is a premise for the reform, that the railway should be a public responsibility. The decision on where to go and on how often to go should continue to be taken by bodies under political control, in order to meet the needs of society and users. Business economic assessments shall not determine where and when the train is to go and the infrastructure shall be owned by the Norwegian State. So it's important that we in the work of facilitating the railway make it more important for more people to take care of what works well today. Then I'd like to mention the most important thing first, namely security. No one can doubt that the safety of Norwegian railways is very good. Also regularity and punctuality are moving in the right direction, with a punctuality achievement of more than 90 pst. in recent years. What, then, is the reason for making changes? Let me mention some key reasons. There is a strong consensus that there is an inappropriate and partly unclear division of tasks within the railway sector today. It is a combination of authority tasks and operational tasks. The railway works are both an authority body and a supplier of the main input factor of the train companies: a well-functioning infrastructure. Responsibility for railway property is another area where there has been a shared ownership between the Railway Agency and Rome Property in the NSB, a division that has created challenges for, for example: node development. The development of the property stock has not been sufficiently viewed in an overall context. Activities have had different objectives and disagreements have arisen. The railway sector currently has weak incentive mechanisms. Today's funding system contains few incentives to keep the railway open for traffic. The railway sector has had too much focus on individual elements of railway policy, such as: linked to specific train departures and departure times. We need an increased customer focus. A railway to be put into service by an increasing number of people and owners of goods in the years to come needs incentives to strengthen customer focus. There is a need for better coordination between rail and other collective activities in metropolitan areas in particular, but also across the rest of the country. There is a clear need to coordinate the ticket system and fares and information across collective services and across county boundaries, so that travel is as seamless as possible for the traveller. In this area, the Ministry of Transport has a responsibility for the development of the necessary tools with a clear allocation of responsibilities and sufficient capacity and competence of those responsible. The establishment of a Directorate directly under the Ministry of Transport transfers to the new Directorate the tasks of authority formerly in the Railway Agency. This will then have a central role in strategic planning, professional support to the Ministry of State Budget, overall responsibility for operation and maintenance, the purchase of transport services and the management of economic instruments. Through the grips that are now being done, it is well-placed for us to be able to think even more completely in the future. The transport system is complex, and everything is related to everything. We need a more comprehensive development of the transport system and the whole transport sector, so that we can make even better use of the different modes of transport, so that it is as seamless as possible for road users. That must be the goal. That is why, in the Christian People's Party, it is an exciting approach on the part of the ministry to assess whether what is now being done can form the basis for establishing a cross-sectoral transport directorate for both rail and road. Then we're not talking about this happening right away. Here we have to take the evolution step by step. But in the long run, you should look at this. In the last growing-up period, the Christian People's Party promoted a proposal for a representative to investigate just a common transport state. Not least in terms of strategic planning to see road and rail in context and to solve the transport challenges of the future, we think it might be a good idea to get through a cross-sectoral directorate. I would also like to stress that although this is an administrative body directly under the Ministry of Transport, it is important that it becomes an actor with a framework and opportunities to initiate and develop new initiatives. There is a need for a directorate that may be at the forefront of development, both in terms of public transport and rail, in view of the increased transport needs that will arise. The establishment of a separate infrastructure undertaking to own, manage, operate, maintain and renew the railway infrastructure, as well as to carry out traffic management and management of property, we believe that a good deal is being done to coordinate and pool tasks that are not within the area of competence. It is important to stress that as part of the new state entity, ROM Property remains a collective, professional property manager, enabling values to be created and to develop collective nodes. This must be done through a comprehensive management of values through interaction with private and public actors. Operational development projects may be sold when the market is present and when the owner receives the best return on capital invested. Whenever one in the notification states that there will be gradual competition for passenger services on trains, it is a question of contributing to the creation of the best possible train service for customers. From the Christian People's Party this is not about ideology, but about the possibility of developing an even better train product for the customer. Train companies have a self-interest in retaining and attracting new customers and developing the travel experience for the traveller. However, it is essential that this is done in stages. It is important that one uses the experience of the competitive outlooks before a next step and that one and one package be out of the way. It should also be a prerequisite that the structure of the new reform is in place before the start of the competitive outsourcing on the first route. For the Christian People's Party, it is important that you still have a strong NSB, a state company that can be a central player in the Norwegian market. It has therefore been essential that NSB AS be continued as a combined transport company consisting of NSB passenger trains, Train Company AB, NSB Gjøvikbanen, Nettbuss, CargoNet and group and joint functions, 100 pst. owned by the State. Opening up the possibility of multiple passenger train operators on the road, as well as opting for the use of several private operators in operation and maintenance, we believe that we can also have a good impact on the development of new solutions. The proposal for a new organisation facilitates better coordination and coordination. The opposition calls the new organisational model "splitting," but I'm going to say that it's actually the opposite that's proposed. A new railway directorate with concentrated planning resources will see the development of infrastructure in the context of future route production and material needs. A new road charging scheme and the introduction of a performance scheme strengthens coordination between infrastructure companies and train operators. A collection of all property in the infrastructure company, where the professional management environment of ROM Property is at the heart of this collective new environment, avoids the type of conflict that has previously occurred, for example: for the development of node functions. Then it is important to add: a reorganization of the railway is not enough to make the railway better and more important for more people. We are very aware that being able to develop the railway to something better than you have today also requires the allocation of financial resources. Over the last two financial years, the four cooperation parties have shown their willingness to invest in the railway through increased investment, operation and maintenance appropriations. For the Christian People's Party, it is vital that this resource effort for the railway should increase in the years ahead. We have a big maintenance run to take it back. For the first time in more than 15 years, we're starting to take up some of the lag in 2015. That was good – that was very good. At the same time, in the Christian People's Party, we experienced talking for deaf ears every time we proposed radical action. We want renewal. It's important that we now think about the future. The railway must become more important for more people. Now the grips that the Christian People's Party has fought for a long time. That's good, and it happens in good cooperation with an offensive government council.

ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-15.ana.ud2166e3238	First, on behalf of the Centre Party, I would like to say that there has been an unfair procedure in this case. Treating such a major and fundamental case in less than four weeks is not a proper procedure. Train transport is important to many, tens of thousands of people commute into and out of our cities by train where there is a train offer. Railways are an efficient and environmentally friendly mode of transport. The centre party believes that the railway must be upgraded and take more of the volume of transport for those using the train for daily travel. Then it is important to develop the train offer also on the slightly longer distances that can build larger regions and parts of the country together. The centre batch will increase the capacity and regularity of freight transport throughout the country. More goods must be placed on the track and on the keel. The centre party believes that the government's proposal to reintroduce a general road tax on the railway will result in even more goods and timber being transported on the road ahead. That's not exactly the direction we want to go. The railway policy must be designed in such a way as to create an attractive and modern train offer for passengers and freight. The aim is that trains should be the preferred option on the routes operated. There will be great demands on those responsible for the track and on those responsible for the train traffic, and there will be a need for good coordination between the two. If the railway is to function optimally, long-term planning is needed, gradually larger investments both in operation and maintenance and in initial investment, and overall thinking. Therefore, the railway sector must have clear political governance and clear requirements. Splitting and market alignment over time has not given the railway sector the necessary promise, and the government will take this development further in the wrong direction. While Norway is now adapting rail to EU competition requirements, resistance has been high in many EU countries. Countries such as France, Italy, Switzerland, Germany, Belgium, Ireland, Luxembourg and Austria have chosen not to comply with EU infrastructure-to-train separation requirements, but have retained an integrated model. Many of these countries are considered the world's leading railway field. Most people also have little competition in passenger traffic. The centre party believes that splitting up Railway Norway even more is the wrong way to go. An integrated solution should be put together between the NSB and the Railway Agency. Research shows that countries with integrated railways consistently perform better results in terms of traffic growth and better train provision than countries that have split up the railways. Sweden and the United Kingdom have gone the opposite way, with a split up of the railways. The Swedish railway system is now characterised by a lot of delays, dissatisfied customers and partly chaotic conditions of operation and maintenance. The UK railway has a very high cost level compared to other European countries and low customer satisfaction. Experience from all European countries is that there are not primarily organisational changes, but the willingness to spend money on the railways that determine whether the railways are going well or not. The railway operation over the last 10 years has produced results. In recent years, train provision has improved significantly, punctuality has increased, fewer trains have been cancelled, many more trips by train, and customer satisfaction has increased. It is important that this bet continues and that the authorities have the opportunity to ensure the quality of the offers offered by the NSB and the Flytrain. Means of operating and maintaining railway infrastructure must be maintained at a high level, so that the maintenance drivage is re-established and eliminated. The centre party wants to keep both the NSB and the Fly train in the state ownership. The NSB must be strengthened and further developed as a modern transport company owned by the State. The superstructure between the Railway Agency and the NSB must be established to give us an integrated model. The main advantage of this is that one is given the opportunity to see infrastructure and passenger services as a whole. Equipment and materials could then be used flexibly and quickly to meet traffic challenges. A clear challenge with competitive outsourcing is that it is shared between different companies, so that it cannot be exploited to the maximum. An integrated company will have the strength and strength to take action quickly and efficiently where necessary. The launch of competition could significantly weaken the offer on the low-traffic routes in this country. It will not be as interesting to drive trains on all sections of Norway. It is likely to be more attractive to operate on the Gardermoban and the most trafficked routes, but not as attractive to all. If the NSB loses the revenues from the most profitable sections of competing companies, there will be less money in the pot currently used by the NSB to make an overall offer, and that is not a good solution. Nor does the centre party authorise the sale of shares in the Flytrain, the company must be state owned – it is a success story from A to Å. This privatisation will affect many of the incredibly skilled and dedicated employees, who will have worse working conditions. That's not a good solution. So the Center Party says yes to a forward-looking railway – and no to fragmentation and privatisation.

We're not doing this because we're ideologically bound to state solutions. We do this because heavy international experience — and Norwegian experience — suggests that funds for the railways in Norway will be used more effectively, unless there is a risk of unnecessary red tape, whether the State concentrates on its part of the job, which is to provide proper funding and state objectives for the NSB and the Railway Agency. Because there is precisely an ideologically-thought-out division between several train companies and even more actors that will not give more railway probability. We don't think it's going to give more railway trouble with more fragmentation and reorganization. The railways must become the backbone of the future Norwegian transport system. It assumes that we decide and plan for the railway to be just that, that we focus on the fact that the journey time between the big cities has to go down, that the capacity is increased, and that the trains go when they're going to. We must then give priority to investing — and not dispersing resources in fragmentation and red tape.

They are sitting and defining how the railway must be in 15 years from population development and the desired transport image. Unfortunately, in Norway we sit more and look at why we do not maintain 15-year-old things instead of seeing 15 years ahead. That's where we have the railway directorate. And when someone then talks about the Norwegian railways being privatised, at least that's not the result of our reform, because in our reform, the railways are to be governed by the public, by politicians, by the state. And then the state has to own everything that's moving on the railway. We collect that in a material company. Where one today is divided into the railway works, a bit on the Fly train and a lot in the NSB, we say that properties, stations, rails and train sets – everything should be the same place. Then there is revenue and expenditure in the same place, then one has the right incentives to make the good investments instead of having different views in the NSB and the Railway Agency on how to dispose of the land in an area. All of this is state-owned. So there's no privatisation going on. We're going to have a long-term planning in the directorate, we're going to have ownership of the material, and we're also going to make sure that what's in the NSB today, a maintenance company, is right under the Ministry of Transport. We also have to own those who maintain the train sets. It's not privatisation. This is about bringing together responsibility, making sure that one gets done something with the slightly random things that have now been lying for too long, and getting things to work better. Then someone has mentioned that the prices of the tickets will go up as a result of this. There are some of the horrors I have registered in this debate. Then I just want to mention that ticket prices will not go up because of the reform. Someone's referring to England. Yes, in England, the railway operation is largely financed by the train customers. In Norway, on the other hand, we have chosen to finance this partly from the State budget. We're using 3 billion. kr this year to subsidise train tickets, precisely because some of the routes would not have a train offer – in fact almost none – if everything were to be self-financed. We want more trains on Norwegian railways, we want more departures, and then we have to make sure that the state is involved and funding this. It doesn't mean higher fares, as claimed. We're maintaining that system as it is today. Then there's someone who claims to get one more than one operator, a few people will have a chaos with ticket types. Well, already today it's like having an NSB ticket, you can't travel with the Flytrain with it. So this problem, as some say, is a challenge with the reform, we're solving -- it was a challenge there, but now we're going to solve it. Some people claim that NSB won't be able to work if someone gets permission to challenge them on their lines. I think that's very defensive. If the goal is a good living environment in Oslo and Akershus and a better effort for Earth's climate, it's not a good idea to pump tens of thousands of extra cars into capital traffic every day. Although several of them will be low-emission cars, they will continue to take the same amount of space from other road users. The Greens support the proposals from the SV and the Left that have been promoted in the vote. It should be obvious that major transport projects in the Oslo area, where large parts of the costs are to be financed by the State, are in line with the climate agreement.

The government’s expenditure in this respect is estimated at 11–12 billion. kr. So you can see this over 30 years, and then you have an investment cost of approximately. 500 million. kr per year. We know that the delay costs per year are estimated at 2 billion. kr. So, with such a simple calculation, it can be seen that the annual socio-economic profitability of this project exceeds the investment cost several times.
